Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you used to enjoy, even if just for a period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any strong way. Like at the senior center meetings, or when I’m sitting through the announcements at church, I get that ants-in-my-chair sort of feeling.\n\nI don’t mean I’m causing a scene or anything—I hope not—but I’ll shift around, or find a reason to get up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me, just to get through the sitting. That’s the main time I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness and need to move, does your heart ever race, or do you feel anxious or worried about having to sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it my heart racing, no… at least I haven’t really noticed that. And I don’t think I’m worried exactly, not like I’m afraid to sit still.\n\nIt’s more just… uncomfortable, like I want to get up and do something. But if there’s a difference you’re looking for, I’m not sure I’m explaining it quite right.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling restless or need to keep moving, do you also find that your thoughts are racing or that you feel like you need to keep talking even if there's no need to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t know that I’d say my thoughts are racing, no. Not in the way that sounds. And I don’t think I have to keep talking… at least I hope I’m not doing that.\n\nI may chat a bit if I’m uncomfortable, just to be pleasant, but I haven’t really noticed it as a need, if that makes sense. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of situations where you have to sit still, do you ever feel physically agitated or like you need to move even when you’re not trying to do anything specific?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m not pacing the floors exactly, but I do seem to always be puttering around—fixing something, arranging things, tidying a drawer, signing up to help with one more little thing.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. For the past year or so, even at my age, I’ve had a hard time just sitting down and being still unless I really make myself. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you used to enjoy, even if just for a period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any strong way. I mean, I have ordinary low days, I suppose everyone does, especially when things are quiet, but I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI haven’t really noticed losing interest in things. If I’m tired or restless I may not settle to them well, but that feels a bit different. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you were not living up to your expectations or felt excessive guilt about things you’ve done or didn’t do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “excessive” guilt, no. I mean, I can be hard on myself in the ordinary way—wishing I’d handled something better or been more organized—but I wouldn’t say I feel weighed down by guilt.\n\nAs for expectations… well, at my age I try not to set the bar quite so high. I suppose I do get a little disappointed with myself now and then, but it doesn’t feel like some terrible failing. Does that answer it properly?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.00, F43.10, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you, Doctor. I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve noticed over the past year or so that I’m just always doing something. Puttering around the house, fixing little things, arranging this or that, saying yes to another volunteer thing. I know that doesn’t sound terrible, and I do like to be useful, but even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't relax or need to keep moving, even when you want to rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is—I’m not causing trouble, I don’t think—but I do seem to always be up doing something. Puttering around, fixing a little thing, arranging something, or saying yes to one more volunteer job.\n\nEven when I tell myself I’m going to sit and rest, I’ll notice something that “just needs a minute,” and then I’m up again. At my age, you’d think I’d be better at being still, wouldn’t you?\n\nDoctor: When you try to sit down and rest, do you feel a sense of inner restlessness or discomfort, like you should be doing something even if there’s nothing that needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I hate to make it sound dramatic. It’s more like, if I’m at a senior center meeting or sitting through the announcements at church, I get this sort of… ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or find a reason to stand up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me—just little things, you know, nothing disruptive, I hope. It just helps me get through sitting there. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is this restlessness or need to move something you feel all the time, or does it happen mostly in certain situations like when you have to sit still for a while?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I have to sit still for a while, I think. Like at the senior center meetings, or when I’m sitting through the announcements at church, I get that ants-in-my-chair sort of feeling.\n\nI don’t mean I’m causing a scene or anything—I hope not—but I’ll shift around, or find a reason to get up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me, just to get through the sitting. That’s the main time I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness and need to move, does your heart ever race, or do you feel anxious or worried about having to sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it my heart racing, no… at least I haven’t really noticed that. And I don’t think I’m worried exactly, not like I’m afraid to sit still.\n\nIt’s more just… uncomfortable, like I want to get up and do something. But if there’s a difference you’re looking for, I’m not sure I’m explaining it quite right.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling restless or need to keep moving, do you also find that your thoughts are racing or that you feel like you need to keep talking even if there's no need to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t know that I’d say my thoughts are racing, no. Not in the way that sounds. And I don’t think I have to keep talking… at least I hope I’m not doing that.\n\nI may chat a bit if I’m uncomfortable, just to be pleasant, but I haven’t really noticed it as a need, if that makes sense. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of situations where you have to sit still, do you ever feel physically agitated or like you need to move even when you’re not trying to do anything specific?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m not pacing the floors exactly, but I do seem to always be puttering around—fixing something, arranging things, tidying a drawer, signing up to help with one more little thing.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. For the past year or so, even at my age, I’ve had a hard time just sitting down and being still unless I really make myself. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you used to enjoy, even if just for a period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any strong way. I mean, I have ordinary low days, I suppose everyone does, especially when things are quiet, but I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI haven’t really noticed losing interest in things. If I’m tired or restless I may not settle to them well, but that feels a bit different. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you were not living up to your expectations or felt excessive guilt about things you’ve done or didn’t do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “excessive” guilt, no. I mean, I can be hard on myself in the ordinary way—wishing I’d handled something better or been more organized—but I wouldn’t say I feel weighed down by guilt.\n\nAs for expectations… well, at my age I try not to set the bar quite so high. I suppose I do get a little disappointed with myself now and then, but it doesn’t feel like some terrible failing. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting more easily irritated or experiencing sudden shifts in mood, even if briefly, during the day?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I can get a little short if I’m tired or if something is taking longer than it should, but I don’t think I have sudden mood swings.\n\nAt least, no one has pointed that out to me. I hope I’m answering that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. My sleep seems about the same, as far as I can tell. If I don’t sleep much, I usually feel it the next day—I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested on very little sleep.\n\nI hope that’s the kind of thing you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed whether the moving around makes it harder to focus, or if I’m just… being a little fidgety.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

At the senior center, after a couple of meetings, I’ve had to apologize because I think I was a bit too fidgety or rushed with people. And one friend did suggest maybe I take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a little tense.

I felt badly about that. I’m not trying to cause trouble. Does that answer what you’re asking?

And one friend did suggest maybe I take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a little tense.\n\nI felt badly about that. I’m not trying to cause trouble. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Last Question]\nDoes the restlessness or need to keep moving ever make it hard for you to enjoy or participate in social activities, or cause any problems at home or with your volunteer work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 87 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At the senior center, I’ve had to apologize after meetings, and one friend suggested I take a smaller role at the sign-up table because my manner was making things tense.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… yes, a little. I don’t think it keeps me from going, exactly—I still go to the senior center and try to help.

But I have had to apologize after a couple of meetings because I think I came across as too fidgety or hurried. And one friend did suggest I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a bit tense.

I felt embarrassed about that. I’m not meaning to bother anyone. Is that the sort of problem you mean?

It’s more like, if I’m at a senior center meeting or sitting through the announcements at church, I get this sort of… ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or find a reason to stand up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me—just little things, you know, nothing disruptive, I hope. It just helps me get through sitting there. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is this restlessness or need to move something you feel all the time, or does it happen mostly in certain situations like when you have to sit still for a while?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I have to sit still for a while, I think. Like at the senior center meetings, or when I’m sitting through the announcements at church, I get that ants-in-my-chair sort of feeling.\n\nI don’t mean I’m causing a scene or anything—I hope not—but I’ll shift around, or find a reason to get up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me, just to get through the sitting. That’s the main time I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness and need to move, does your heart ever race, or do you feel anxious or worried about having to sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it my heart racing, no… at least I haven’t really noticed that. And I don’t think I’m worried exactly, not like I’m afraid to sit still.\n\nIt’s more just… uncomfortable, like I want to get up and do something. But if there’s a difference you’re looking for, I’m not sure I’m explaining it quite right.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling restless or need to keep moving, do you also find that your thoughts are racing or that you feel like you need to keep talking even if there's no need to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t know that I’d say my thoughts are racing, no. Not in the way that sounds. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of situations where you have to sit still, do you ever feel physically agitated or like you need to move even when you’re not trying to do anything specific?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m not pacing the floors exactly, but I do seem to always be puttering around—fixing something, arranging things, tidying a drawer, signing up to help with one more little thing.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you used to enjoy, even if just for a period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any strong way. I mean, I have ordinary low days, I suppose everyone does, especially when things are quiet, but I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI haven’t really noticed losing interest in things. If I’m tired or restless I may not settle to them well, but that feels a bit different. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you were not living up to your expectations or felt excessive guilt about things you’ve done or didn’t do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “excessive” guilt, no. I mean, I can be hard on myself in the ordinary way—wishing I’d handled something better or been more organized—but I wouldn’t say I feel weighed down by guilt.\n\nAs for expectations… well, at my age I try not to set the bar quite so high. I suppose I do get a little disappointed with myself now and then, but it doesn’t feel like some terrible failing. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting more easily irritated or experiencing sudden shifts in mood, even if briefly, during the day?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I can get a little short if I’m tired or if something is taking longer than it should, but I don’t think I have sudden mood swings.\n\nAt least, no one has pointed that out to me. I hope I’m answering that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. It has been going on for a while, yes, at least several weeks. I hope that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling restless or need to move, do you also find it hard to focus or concentrate on what's being said or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Hyperactive/Impulsive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped going or anything like that… I still do enjoy the senior center.

But I do feel a bit upset afterward sometimes, especially when I’ve had to apologize after meetings. And that friend suggesting I take a smaller role at the sign-up table—because I was making things tense—well, that embarrassed me. I suppose that made me pull back a little there, even if I didn’t mean to.

Is that the kind of thing you mean?

At my age, you’d think I’d be better at being still, wouldn’t you?\n\nDoctor: When you try to sit down and rest, do you feel a sense of inner restlessness or discomfort, like you should be doing something even if there’s nothing that needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I hate to make it sound dramatic. It just helps me get through sitting there. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is this restlessness or need to move something you feel all the time, or does it happen mostly in certain situations like when you have to sit still for a while?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I have to sit still for a while, I think. Like at the senior center meetings, or when I’m sitting through the announcements at church, I get that ants-in-my-chair sort of feeling.\n\nI don’t mean I’m causing a scene or anything—I hope not—but I’ll shift around, or find a reason to get up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me, just to get through the sitting. That’s the main time I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness and need to move, does your heart ever race, or do you feel anxious or worried about having to sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it my heart racing, no… at least I haven’t really noticed that. And I don’t think I’m worried exactly, not like I’m afraid to sit still.\n\nIt’s more just… uncomfortable, like I want to get up and do something. But if there’s a difference you’re looking for, I’m not sure I’m explaining it quite right.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling restless or need to keep moving, do you also find that your thoughts are racing or that you feel like you need to keep talking even if there's no need to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t know that I’d say my thoughts are racing, no. Not in the way that sounds. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of situations where you have to sit still, do you ever feel physically agitated or like you need to move even when you’re not trying to do anything specific?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m not pacing the floors exactly, but I do seem to always be puttering around—fixing something, arranging things, tidying a drawer, signing up to help with one more little thing.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I don’t think it keeps me from going, exactly—I still go to the senior center and try to help.\n\nBut I have had to apologize after a couple of meetings because I think I came across as too fidgety or hurried. And one friend did suggest I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a bit tense.\n\nI felt embarrassed about that. I’m not meaning to bother anyone. Is that the sort of problem you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed or upset specifically because of this restlessness, or has it caused you to avoid or withdraw from certain activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped going or anything like that… I still do enjoy the senior center.\n\nBut I do feel a bit upset afterward sometimes, especially when I’ve had to apologize after meetings. And that friend suggesting I take a smaller role at the sign-up table—because I was making things tense—well, that embarrassed me. I suppose that made me pull back a little there, even if I didn’t mean to.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're not in a situation where you have to sit still or when there's no specific task to do, do you still feel a need to move, like you can't settle your body even when you want to relax?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. It’s not dramatic, I don’t think, but even when there isn’t something I have to do, I tend to be up and puttering around—fixing something, arranging things, finding one more little task.

That’s part of why I came in, really. For the past year or so, I feel like I’m always moving on to the next thing, or volunteering for one more thing, and even at my age I have a hard time just sitting down and being still. I can do it for a bit, but it doesn’t feel natural, if that makes sense.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.00, F43.10, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you, Doctor. I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve noticed over the past year or so that I’m just always doing something. Puttering around the house, fixing little things, arranging this or that, saying yes to another volunteer thing. I know that doesn’t sound terrible, and I do like to be useful, but even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't relax or need to keep moving, even when you want to rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is—I’m not causing trouble, I don’t think—but I do seem to always be up doing something. Puttering around, fixing a little thing, arranging something, or saying yes to one more volunteer job.\n\nEven when I tell myself I’m going to sit and rest, I’ll notice something that “just needs a minute,” and then I’m up again. At my age, you’d think I’d be better at being still, wouldn’t you?\n\nDoctor: When you try to sit down and rest, do you feel a sense of inner restlessness or discomfort, like you should be doing something even if there’s nothing that needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I hate to make it sound dramatic. It’s more like, if I’m at a senior center meeting or sitting through the announcements at church, I get this sort of… ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or find a reason to stand up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me—just little things, you know, nothing disruptive, I hope. It just helps me get through sitting there. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is this restlessness or need to move something you feel all the time, or does it happen mostly in certain situations like when you have to sit still for a while?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I have to sit still for a while, I think. Like at the senior center meetings, or when I’m sitting through the announcements at church, I get that ants-in-my-chair sort of feeling.\n\nI don’t mean I’m causing a scene or anything—I hope not—but I’ll shift around, or find a reason to get up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me, just to get through the sitting. That’s the main time I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness and need to move, does your heart ever race, or do you feel anxious or worried about having to sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it my heart racing, no… at least I haven’t really noticed that. And I don’t think I’m worried exactly, not like I’m afraid to sit still.\n\nIt’s more just… uncomfortable, like I want to get up and do something. But if there’s a difference you’re looking for, I’m not sure I’m explaining it quite right.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling restless or need to keep moving, do you also find that your thoughts are racing or that you feel like you need to keep talking even if there's no need to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t know that I’d say my thoughts are racing, no. Not in the way that sounds. And I don’t think I have to keep talking… at least I hope I’m not doing that.\n\nI may chat a bit if I’m uncomfortable, just to be pleasant, but I haven’t really noticed it as a need, if that makes sense. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of situations where you have to sit still, do you ever feel physically agitated or like you need to move even when you’re not trying to do anything specific?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m not pacing the floors exactly, but I do seem to always be puttering around—fixing something, arranging things, tidying a drawer, signing up to help with one more little thing.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. For the past year or so, even at my age, I’ve had a hard time just sitting down and being still unless I really make myself. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you used to enjoy, even if just for a period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any strong way. I mean, I have ordinary low days, I suppose everyone does, especially when things are quiet, but I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI haven’t really noticed losing interest in things. If I’m tired or restless I may not settle to them well, but that feels a bit different. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you were not living up to your expectations or felt excessive guilt about things you’ve done or didn’t do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “excessive” guilt, no. I mean, I can be hard on myself in the ordinary way—wishing I’d handled something better or been more organized—but I wouldn’t say I feel weighed down by guilt.\n\nAs for expectations… well, at my age I try not to set the bar quite so high. I suppose I do get a little disappointed with myself now and then, but it doesn’t feel like some terrible failing. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting more easily irritated or experiencing sudden shifts in mood, even if briefly, during the day?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I can get a little short if I’m tired or if something is taking longer than it should, but I don’t think I have sudden mood swings.\n\nAt least, no one has pointed that out to me. I hope I’m answering that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. My sleep seems about the same, as far as I can tell. If I don’t sleep much, I usually feel it the next day—I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested on very little sleep.\n\nI hope that’s the kind of thing you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of restlessness or need to move is something that happens most days, and has been going on like this for at least several weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose most days, in little ways. It’s not dramatic, I don’t think, but if I’m sitting too long—like at the senior center meetings or during church announcements—I get this sort of ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or I’ll get up for coffee, or straighten papers, just something to move a bit. It has been going on for a while, yes, at least several weeks. I hope that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling restless or need to move, do you also find it hard to focus or concentrate on what's being said or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed whether the moving around makes it harder to focus, or if I’m just… being a little fidgety.\n\nI can usually follow along well enough, I think. If I miss something, I’m not always sure if it’s because I was restless or just because my mind wandered a bit like anyone’s does. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does this restlessness ever interfere with your ability to do things you need to do or cause problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nAt the senior center, after a couple of meetings, I’ve had to apologize because I think I was a bit too fidgety or rushed with people. And one friend did suggest maybe I take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a little tense.\n\nI felt badly about that. I’m not trying to cause trouble. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Does the restlessness or need to keep moving ever make it hard for you to enjoy or participate in social activities, or cause any problems at home or with your volunteer work?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, a little. I’m not meaning to bother anyone. And I don’t think I’m worried exactly, not like I’m afraid to sit still.\n\nIt’s more just… uncomfortable, like I want to get up and do something. But if there’s a difference you’re looking for, I’m not sure I’m explaining it quite right.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling restless or need to keep moving, do you also find that your thoughts are racing or that you feel like you need to keep talking even if there's no need to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t know that I’d say my thoughts are racing, no. Not in the way that sounds. And I don’t think I have to keep talking… at least I hope I’m not doing that.\n\nI may chat a bit if I’m uncomfortable, just to be pleasant, but I haven’t really noticed it as a need, if that makes sense. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of situations where you have to sit still, do you ever feel physically agitated or like you need to move even when you’re not trying to do anything specific?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m not pacing the floors exactly, but I do seem to always be puttering around—fixing something, arranging things, tidying a drawer, signing up to help with one more little thing.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. For the past year or so, even at my age, I’ve had a hard time just sitting down and being still unless I really make myself. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you used to enjoy, even if just for a period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any strong way. I mean, I have ordinary low days, I suppose everyone does, especially when things are quiet, but I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI haven’t really noticed losing interest in things. If I’m tired or restless I may not settle to them well, but that feels a bit different. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you were not living up to your expectations or felt excessive guilt about things you’ve done or didn’t do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “excessive” guilt, no. I mean, I can be hard on myself in the ordinary way—wishing I’d handled something better or been more organized—but I wouldn’t say I feel weighed down by guilt.\n\nAs for expectations… well, at my age I try not to set the bar quite so high. I suppose I do get a little disappointed with myself now and then, but it doesn’t feel like some terrible failing. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting more easily irritated or experiencing sudden shifts in mood, even if briefly, during the day?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I can get a little short if I’m tired or if something is taking longer than it should, but I don’t think I have sudden mood swings.\n\nAt least, no one has pointed that out to me. I hope I’m answering that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. My sleep seems about the same, as far as I can tell. If I don’t sleep much, I usually feel it the next day—I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested on very little sleep.\n\nI hope that’s the kind of thing you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of restlessness or need to move is something that happens most days, and has been going on like this for at least several weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose most days, in little ways. It’s not dramatic, I don’t think, but if I’m sitting too long—like at the senior center meetings or during church announcements—I get this sort of ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or I’ll get up for coffee, or straighten papers, just something to move a bit. It has been going on for a while, yes, at least several weeks. I hope that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling restless or need to move, do you also find it hard to focus or concentrate on what's being said or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed whether the moving around makes it harder to focus, or if I’m just… being a little fidgety.\n\nI can usually follow along well enough, I think. If I miss something, I’m not always sure if it’s because I was restless or just because my mind wandered a bit like anyone’s does. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does this restlessness ever interfere with your ability to do things you need to do or cause problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nAt the senior center, after a couple of meetings, I’ve had to apologize because I think I was a bit too fidgety or rushed with people. And one friend did suggest maybe I take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a little tense.\n\nI felt badly about that. I’m not trying to cause trouble. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Does the restlessness or need to keep moving ever make it hard for you to enjoy or participate in social activities, or cause any problems at home or with your volunteer work?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, a little. I don’t think it keeps me from going, exactly—I still go to the senior center and try to help.\n\nBut I have had to apologize after a couple of meetings because I think I came across as too fidgety or hurried. And one friend did suggest I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a bit tense.\n\nI felt embarrassed about that. I’m not meaning to bother anyone. Is that the sort of problem you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed or upset specifically because of this restlessness, or has it caused you to avoid or withdraw from certain activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped going or anything like that… I still do enjoy the senior center.\n\nBut I do feel a bit upset afterward sometimes, especially when I’ve had to apologize after meetings. And that friend suggesting I take a smaller role at the sign-up table—because I was making things tense—well, that embarrassed me. I suppose that made me pull back a little there, even if I didn’t mean to.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you're not in a situation where you have to sit still or when there's no specific task to do, do you still feel a need to move, like you can't settle your body even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not dramatic, I don’t think, but even when there isn’t something I have to do, I tend to be up and puttering around—fixing something, arranging things, finding one more little task.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, really. For the past year or so, I feel like I’m always moving on to the next thing, or volunteering for one more thing, and even at my age I have a hard time just sitting down and being still. I can do it for a bit, but it doesn’t feel natural, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness affected your ability to function in any important areas of your life, such as your ability to enjoy time with family, participate fully in volunteer work, or carry out your usual daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I’d say it’s been fairly mild, I think. The main place I’ve noticed it is at the senior center.\n\nAfter a couple of meetings I did have to apologize, because I think my manner came across as a little too hurried or fidgety. And one friend suggested I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table because I was making things feel tense.\n\nI felt badly about that. I’m not trying to be difficult, of course. But yes, I suppose it has affected things a bit there."
